Server report: Jul 19 – Jul 26, 2026

Launch week for the ASGC Game Jam 2026: 400+ jammers arrive, ClickUp buckles, and Project Atlantis starts taking shape underwater

6952 messages · 259 active members · 167 active channels

This was public onboarding week for the ASGC Community Game Jam (Project Atlantis), and it was busy. Nearly 7,000 messages came from 259 active members. #general and the discipline channels filled with introductions from composers, writers, programmers, artists, designers, QA testers, a user researcher and a localization specialist. Moderators and helpers (DeeJuice, NamasteGeek, Michal Fried, biestibelzer, ThePokePreston) steered newcomers through the start-here roadmap toward their blue-name role. Getting access was the week's main struggle. ClickUp invites were sent by hand and often showed 'page not available', a Drive restructure briefly broke doc links, and around 400 people had to be added to GitHub teams. Volunteers and leads answered with public read-only wiki links, a self-service GitHub invite site and GitHub SSO on the Gitea LFS server.

On the production side, leads settled on a single pipeline: GDD → TDD → ClickUp Epic → GitHub Issues, with ClickUp and GitHub as the only sources of truth and Miro used only for high-level communication. Unreal 5.7.4 is the official engine, Git LFS file locking is live, and a Build and Release crew (Ricchio, AaronBurrSir, VibeBlind) is working toward an internal Steamworks depot upload rather than a public store page. Sprint 1 is documentation-heavy and leads into the Dev phase starting August 1. The week also brought real friction. Narrative raised concerns about late communication and a proposed scope-down to a single level, which led to an MVP-plus-stretch-goals compromise discussion and a scope call still to come. Art lead Garrit stepped away, leads were retitled Directors, the embedded mentor program was cancelled, and on Jul 24 ASGC announced it had removed Daniel Beahn from the project.

Creative work still got going. Designers opened feature threads for Oxygen, Traversal, Combat, Journal, Dialogue and Investigation, and argued over how oxygen fits the 'cozy' pillar. Audio held its first sync and heard Nate's well-received world-instrument style sketch. QA held its meet-and-greet and set its July 31 deliverables. Narrative drafted its writing pods. On Saturday the art team launched a relay-drawn prototype loading screen, passing one PSD from artist to artist to test its asset submission workflow.

Themes

  • Onboarding and access growing pains — ClickUp invites were sent manually per department, and everyone joined as a Limited Member, which caused widespread 'page not available' errors and roughly 131 pending invites. YosephAlex4nder, Cafedunoir and Swart Tigerhorn shared public doc.clickup.com links as a workaround. Michal Fried and Sidds moved Drive into lead-only and public folders after private info was found to be exposed. That briefly locked out several leads, including VibeBlind from his own docs. Crev reported broken onboarding steps, and the Google Form for ClickUp access failed for people across all departments. (#🎸│general, #⁉️general-questions⁉️, #ClickUp Google Form Access Error, #🗓️│production-lead-discussion, #🎯│leads-discussion, #Updates on Google Drive, #❔narrative-questions❔, #📌│production-status-update)
  • Tech pipeline and tooling decisions — Unreal 5.7.4 was confirmed as the official engine (ADR 001), with Blueprints first and C++ where needed. Tech tasks live in GitHub Projects. Git LFS locks run through Gitea (asgc.freemyip.com), where Adrian set up GitHub SSO and fixed team permissions after Zalk's 401/409 locking errors. pullipulli's invite bot took over adding ~400 people to GitHub teams. Adrian's PR #6 set up Content folders following the Allar UE5 style guide. VibeBlind announced a planned SDET team to help offset QA being heavily outnumbered by Tech. (#🎯│tech-discussions, #❔tech-questions❔, #💻│tech-lead-discussion, #Add some 400 peeps to GitHub, #File locking w/ git lfs, #I am having some issues testing locking)
  • Build, release and QA setup — The Sprint 1 tech deliverable is an executable in a Steam Depot that launches and shows a static image. A public store release is 'like next year', since Steam needs a 'Coming Soon' page up for two weeks first. Ricchio took ownership of the build pipeline and opened GitHub issues #10 and #11. QA confirmed weekly builds and GitHub Issues as the bug tracker, plans at least one QA member per pod, and committed to test plan and bug template deliverables by July 31. (#Build Producer and Release Producer, #🪲│qa-lead-discussion, #🎯│qa-discussions, #📢│qa-announcements, #Sprint#1 announcement, #Task Workflow)
  • Scope, structure and leadership changes — Leslie (Oldladies7) raised concerns about late process information and the one-level scope-down. The narrative voice channel worried that 50 writers would have too little to do. Daniel framed scope as a one-level MVP with stretch goals, while Leslie proposed three levels (big, medium, small), and a scope call is pending. Xelnath clarified that the jam means '6 weeks of scope in 6 months', and gave its priorities as learning first and portfolio second. Leads were retitled Directors, team switching locks after Sprint 3, the mentor program was cancelled, art lead Garrit stepped away, and ASGC removed Daniel Beahn on Jul 24. (#Production alignment, #🎯│director-discussion, #📝│narrative-lead-discussion, #narrative-voice-channel, #🎯│production-discussions)
  • Designing a cozy underwater steampunk mystery — Feature threads explored how oxygen can stay 'cozy'. Ideas included expanding breathable zones, stamina-like drain, deflating balloon-tank diegetic UI and no teleport resets. Traversal settled on a Scaphander-style suit that sinks by default and spends oxygen to swim or float. Combat is optional and non-lethal under the pillar 'Clever, not hard', and the Journal brainstorm proposed a two-in-one tablet plus paper notebook. Audio, art and narrative members brainstormed world instruments, hydrothermal vents, steampunk prosthetics for sea life and a utopia with a hidden secret. (#🎯│design-discussions, #FDS-003 - Oxygen System, #FDS-001 - Traversal (Player Movement), #FDS-009 - Combat, #FDS-006 - Journal Feature, #🎯│audio-discussions, #🎯│art-discussions, #I have been thinking about the game)
  • Discipline kickoffs and community bonding — Each department held its first gatherings. Audio's sync took place Jul 24, QA's meet-and-greet ran the same day, and Art's onboarding call led straight into the relay loading screen sprint. Narrative drafted pods of 4–6 writers and set Sunday 1pm PT leads meetings. Dozens of intro channels turned into friendships over TTRPGs, martial arts, cats, terrariums, impostor syndrome and a planned Gamescom meetup. (#SPRINT 1: Prototype Loading Screen!, #art-voice-channel, #Sorting Hat Pod Decisions, #Standard Meeting Times, #Eve Pioche - Community and Support lead with a writer's hat, #Laura Habers - Narrative Designer/Writer)

Shoutouts

  • pullipulli — Built the self-service GitHub intake/invite site (asgcgithub.pulli.me), which spared volunteers from adding ~400 members to GitHub teams by hand, and found the Gitea local-password fix for LFS auth.
  • Adrian — Set up GitHub SSO on the Gitea LFS server, debugged Zalk's locking failures down to the root cause, added all Devs to the Gitea org team, and opened PR #6 for the content structure.
  • YosephAlex4nder — Migrated Narrative's ClickUp into the ASGC workspace, posted public read-only wiki links that unblocked people across the server, and wrote clear handoff and backup documentation.
  • Michal Fried — Built the onboarding roadmap and department start-here flows, and caught and locked down publicly exposed participant data in Drive.
  • Zef — Welcomed nearly every new artist, launched the relay-drawn loading screen sprint, and fixed the PSD bit depth so it would open in Sketchbook and Procreate.

Channels

#🎯│tech-discussions

590 messages from 44 people

The week kicked off with a big wave of introductions as the tech department came online. voymasa, Xhemoz and Dione shared their backgrounds, and VibeBlind (Seth, the tech lead, CST) moved intros into a new Introductions forum. Onboarding was bumpy. ClickUp invites were slow, expired or showed an empty or unavailable board. ThePokePreston said this was expected while permissions roll out. Swart Tigerhorn tested access to the Tech Design docs and shared public read-only doc.clickup.com links. pullipulli suggested swapping the link in tech-status-update for the public one, and VibeBlind made the change. VibeBlind clarified that ClickUp will mostly hold high-level tasks for leads, while tech tasks will live on GitHub Projects. His reasons: it's free, it suits open source, and it attaches public names to issues. He shared a Tech Welcome video and a "Click This!" onboarding tool built by Carlos Ortiz. When it turned out all 400+ members had to be added to GitHub teams by hand, volunteers stepped up, and pullipulli built a GitHub intake site (asgcgithub.pulli.me). VibeBlind also posted a When2Meet; Hanhula pointed out it needed the timezone option enabled, and he fixed it.

On process, VibeBlind asked for Build and Release managers (1 owner, 2 co-owners plus shadows) to ship to GitHub and Steam. snakeytiger, Xandemon, nicholas, pullipulli and francolovesgames showed interest. Xandemon shared Epic's Unreal container images for CI/CD. VibeBlind shared team stats: about 70 programmers against only 8 QA, so some devs will learn SDET work. He also floated alternating sprints between feature work and build stabilization to fight tech debt, and confirmed the rule that you can't review your own code. Guidance agreed on: prototype in Blueprints, then refactor into C++ when it makes sense. He explained that the tech groupings are communities of practice for mentorship and reviews, separate from feature pods. He published a draft Tech Org spreadsheet, defining "Strong" as roughly 3+ years of professional work and defaulting to junior when unsure. Swart Tigerhorn said Tech Design will likely be called on first, probably Monday, since many GDDs are coming in next week. Side threads covered Rider vs Visual Studio (Rider was the clear favorite), BP vs C++, uint/enums over bool, and lots of university nostalgia.

Highlights - Carlos Ortiz built the tech onboarding tool, and VibeBlind shared a Tech Welcome video. - ClickUp access was broken or empty for many people; producers said this was expected while permissions roll out, and Swart Tigerhorn shared public read-only doc links. - VibeBlind confirmed tech tasks will live on GitHub Projects, with ClickUp kept mainly for leads' high-level tasks. - pullipulli built a GitHub team intake site (asgcgithub.pulli.me) after it emerged that 400+ people had to be added by hand. - VibeBlind is recruiting Build and Release managers for shipping to GitHub and Steam; Xandemon pointed to Epic's Unreal container images for CI/CD. - Tech has about 70 programmers but QA has only 8, so some devs will learn SDET work; VibeBlind wants to alternate feature and stabilization sprints to prevent tech debt. - A draft Tech Org spreadsheet was published, with communities of practice kept separate from feature pods. - Swart Tigerhorn expects Tech Design volunteers to be called on as early as Monday, with many GDDs arriving next week.

Topics: Introductions, Onboarding & ClickUp access, GitHub Projects & team intake, Build/Release pipeline, Tech org chart, Blueprints vs C++, IDE preferences (Rider vs VS), Testing, QA & tech debt, University experiences

Open questions - Can members join other departments' meetings, and where are meeting times posted? A stage was set up, but no schedule was shared. - Can the free non-commercial JetBrains Rider license be used for this project? (WaffleFish, nicholas) - Are there recommended Rider plugins for Unreal? (voymasa) Only a recommended-settings link was shared. - Who will own the Build and Release manager roles? The sign-up doc is still pending. - How do pods take tasks, and is ClickUp needed for that? (pullipulli) - Osiris asked what to do next after accepting the ClickUp invite. - Why can't users see the Tech docs inside ClickUp itself, rather than only through public links? (voymasa) - dyzzy asked to be added to deputy leads in the org sheet.

#🎸│general

558 messages from 138 people

This was launch week for the ASGC community game jam server, and #general filled up fast. Moderators and helpers kept steering newcomers through onboarding: DeeJuice, NamasteGeek, Michal Fried, biestibelzer and ThePokePreston repeatedly pointed people to #➡️start-here⬅️ and then their discipline-specific start-here. Finishing it earns the 'roadmap confirmed' role and a blue name, and those unlock the department channels. There were early hiccups. Crev reported onboarding step 8→9 was broken and that the last step linked back to step 8. Ducks found the invite email had landed in Outlook junk. Several people had trouble with the ClickUp access Google Form and later with ClickUp invites or errors. shadowarrior said ClickUp invites would start rolling out (m299), reposted the form link and opened a thread to track affected people. Later in the week RicSan noted the ClickUp errors were happening to everyone while setup continued. Members were asked to set their server name to their real/LinkedIn name, ideally in the format 'FIRST LAST Department'. On Wednesday ThePokePreston mass-pinged everyone who hadn't finished onboarding.

Xelnath clarified a few things. Technical artists should start in the Tech roadmap, and tech art/tech design dual roles will come later with sign-off from both discipline leads. Mentors aren't active yet, and everyone should finish onboarding this week. The rest of the week was a wave of introductions from composers, narrative designers, programmers, 3D/2D artists, QA testers, a user researcher and a localization specialist, plus nervous-but-excited chatter. Social threads followed: a pet-picture thread that biestibelzer started, a fighting-game discussion (Smash, Rivals of Aether 2, 2XKO, Yomi Hustle), Deadlock hangouts, and a G4C meetup shout-out. By Saturday the art team was discussing an art 'sprint' task with a proposed final day of 7/29, and members confirmed that GIMP can open and export PSD files.

Highlights - Crev reported broken onboarding: step 8→9 showed a locked channel and the last step looped back to step 8; Michal Fried took it on. - Xelnath: technical artists should start in Tech; tech art/tech design dual roles will be supported later after both discipline leads sign off. - Xelnath: mentors aren't active in the server yet, so please complete onboarding this week. - shadowarrior announced ClickUp access requests were being processed with invites starting the next day, and opened a thread for people whose Google Form access failed. - ThePokePreston mass-pinged everyone without a blue name/role and set the naming format 'FIRST LAST Department'. - Dozens of members introduced themselves, from composer Tsering (Kingdom Hearts fan) to user researcher Raymond Dosono (Fortnite, Borderlands). - NamasteGeek celebrated getting Unreal installed on a Mac, the week's most-reacted message (32). - For the art sprint task, Smetch_D gave 7/29 as the proposed final day, and several artists confirmed GIMP handles PSD files.

Topics: Onboarding & roles, ClickUp access issues, Server naming & timezones, Introductions, Discipline switching & tech art, Pet pictures, Fighting games, Art sprint / PSD tools

Open questions - Has the broken onboarding flow (step 8→9 locked channel, last step looping to step 8) been fixed? - Osiris asked how to get Git repo access after filling the Google Form; no direct answer was given. - sevenn4 asked whether registering with Outlook but using Gmail for the ClickUp form is an issue; it was only partially answered (use the same account if possible). - sevenn4 asked for recommendations on a platform to journal their game jam experience; they were redirected but never got an answer. - When will ClickUp pages actually be viewable and functional for members (cabinetchimera's error)? - ShifuDaxiongmao suggested everyone add a UTC offset to their names; this was not formally adopted.

#🎯│design-discussions

354 messages from 47 people

A busy onboarding week in #design-discussions. Dozens of new designers introduced themselves across systems, level, UI/UX, tech design, narrative and user research. Many are industry veterans from studios like Obsidian, Blizzard, Epic, ArenaNet, Bohemia and Battlefield 6's Portal team, alongside many recent grads. Matt Barnes confirmed the design AI stance that David "AssassinCS" Thompson drafted for the wiki: AI is OK for documentation help and throwaway prototypes isolated from the repo, but never for shipped content. Matt also said there are no multiplayer plans. Access was the main pain point: the GDD briefly went private, the Design ClickUp form was missing, and ClickUp permissions were slow. klight shared Keystone as a doc wiki and built a Design Team Organization sheet, and David posted a new ClickUp request form. Swart Tigerhorn (Andre) shared the UI/UX and Tech Design wiki pages, explained what tech design involves, and said about 8 pods are expected next week, needing at least 8 tech designers.

On the design side, Matt opened feature threads for Oxygen, Journal, Dialogue, Combat, Stealth, Resource Economy and Investigation. A lively debate asked whether oxygen and suffocation fit the 'cozy' pillar. Ideas included generous oxygen, tethers or a backpack drone that tows you back, oxygen that only drains while moving, and diegetic HUD gauges. Corvid Quill clarified that combat will be optional action combat under the pillar "Clever, not hard," with Zelda as a strong influence. Corvid also explained that features are owned by leads and pods, so new feature pitches should wait for a proper suggestion channel. Daniel Beahn plans an ideas/feedback form plus an anonymous suggestion box. The week ended with Corvid's When2Meet for a first full design meeting, targeted for Wednesday.

Highlights - Matt Barnes confirmed the AI policy: AI is OK for documentation and early prototypes, can't be used for content, and anything AI should be replaced with human work (m4). - Matt Barnes said there are no multiplayer features planned 'at the moment' (m7). - Matt Barnes explained the feature-owner model: designers own features and pull in Points of Contact from other disciplines (m45). - Matt Barnes created discussion threads for FDS-003 Oxygen, 006 Journal, 008 Dialogue, 009 Combat, 010 Stealth, 013 Resource Economy and 005 Investigation (m147-m154). - A cozy-vs-oxygen debate produced ideas like tethers back to oxygen zones, a 'Lakitu' backpack drone and movement-only oxygen drain (m210, m222, m264). - Corvid Quill clarified combat is optional action combat under the pillar "Clever, not hard," with Zelda as a strong influence (m229, m234, m238). - Daniel Beahn will build a guided ideas/feedback form and an anonymous suggestion box (m175). - Swart Tigerhorn said about 8 pods are expected next week and asked for at least 8 tech designers (m321).

Topics: Introductions, AI usage policy, Tool and doc access (ClickUp, Drive, Keystone, Git), Design Team Organization sheet, Feature discussion threads, Oxygen system and cozy pillar, Combat direction, Feedback/suggestion process, Tech Design vs UI/UX roles, Cross-department channel visibility, Game inspiration recommendations, Meeting scheduling

Open questions - Kurezar, Son of Space, binarie and Jorge Navas filled out the ClickUp form but got no invite; should they resubmit? (m112, m174) - Will design members get visibility into Tech channels, or will a cross-department 'design-general' channel be created? (m122, m127) - What should individual contributors be doing right now beyond playing reference games and joining threads? (m191) - Should there be a thread defining how 'underwater steampunk' tech works, such as power generation and oxygen tanks? #❔tech-questions❔

328 messages from 36 people

A busy onboarding week in #❔tech-questions❔ for the ASGC game jam tech team. Alan H. Diniz confirmed Unreal Engine 5.7.4 as the version to install (not 5.8; VibeBlind pointed to ADR 001 in the ADRs doc for the reasoning), and explained that the team will lean on Blueprints, using C++ where it's needed. That kicked off a long, friendly Blueprints-vs-C++ discussion with DemonseedElite, Awesomeosity, Jake, nicholas and Hanhula. The consensus: use both, prototype and iterate in BP, and move complex or performance-critical code to C++ exposed to BP. Awesomeosity also shared a handy rundown of Unreal smart pointers. VibeBlind set expectations: tech is in PreProd waiting on Design, there will be roughly 2 weeks of self-study (First Hour series, style guides in the repo), and the jam runs 6 months, so it will be slow at first. For now the only ask is to clone the repo, run the template without errors and fill out the Intake. Members who want something to do can switch teams during PreProd.

Access and tooling were the main pain points. ClickUp access was delayed and invite links expired or showed 'no access'. VibeBlind later clarified that ClickUp is only for the wiki (doc link shared), not for tasks. Git LFS file locking is in place, with binaries stored on Gitea (asgc.freemyip.com). Several people (Osiris, voymasa, CaptainSoso) got stuck in credential-manager prompts during git lfs locks until they followed the Gitea setup: log in with GitHub, then set a password. VibeBlind shared an updated SETUP.md in the repo. Linux users (Blight, Alan) hit Wayland and Vulkan SM6 problems with Unreal. Zalk's infinite login loop on git lfs lock was handed to Adrian at the end of the week.

Highlights - Unreal Engine 5.7.4 is the official version; 5.8 was skipped for stability and plugin reasons (ADR 001). - Tech will focus on Blueprints, with C++ where needed; Alan offered to run C++ learning with interested members such as Matteo and CoffeeAddict. - VibeBlind: about 2 weeks of self-study before tasks while Design finishes PreProd, and a reminder that the jam lasts 6 months. - Awesomeosity posted a concise guide to TObjectPtr, TSoftObjectPtr and TWeakObjectPtr. - VibeBlind set up Git LFS file locks, with binaries stored on Gitea; CaptainSoso posted the steps for setting a Gitea password to get past credential prompts. - ClickUp is only needed for the wiki, not tasks; VibeBlind shared the doc link and an updated SETUP.md in the repo. - Linux users ran into Wayland and Vulkan SM6 issues (no Nanite or virtual shadow maps); Alan suggested the game may not need Unreal's fancier features. - Zalk's infinite credential loop when running git lfs lock was escalated to Adrian.

Topics: Unreal 5.7.4 version choice, Blueprints vs C++, Unreal smart pointers, Git LFS and file locking, Gitea credentials setup, ClickUp access, Linux/Vulkan compatibility, Learning resources, PreProd timeline

Open questions - Will DX11 be supported, or is DX12 required for the project? (voymasa) - How will exclusive locking scale for a 400+ person project to avoid merge conflicts on binary assets? (dbugslayer, DemonseedElite) - Can the shared learning links be pinned? (dbugslayer) - Zalk's infinite login loop on git lfs lock is still unresolved (Adrian is on it). - When will pods or team selection happen? (Jacob Braunhut) - Where are the C++-for-C#-developers resources? VibeBlind said they should be in onboarding.

Notable messages

TObjectPtr<T> is ideal for when you need to store a pointer to something else in an object, such as storing a reference to a component in an Actor. TSoftObjectPtr<T> is a weak pointer that points to some object that needs to be loaded (assets, such as SFX, data, etc.) to save on time and memory TWeakObjectPtr<T> is best when you need to capture information in lambda functions, loops.

— Awesomeosity in #❔tech-questions❔ (jump)

As someone who studied computer science and loves performance, it's really hard to beat the speed of prototyping that you get from blueprints. Never be afraid to use whatever system you’re comfortable with!

— Jake in #❔tech-questions❔ (jump)

Guys, you don't have to do anything yet. Just make sure you can clone the repo and play the template without errors. ClickUp will come soon. I'm not sure what the hiccup is there, but I promise you that I'll let you know when I have things for you guys to do

— VibeBlind in #❔tech-questions❔ (jump)

Yeah, I've currently got a system set up in BP that loads a toooonne of different data assets in at the same time and then holds them in memory. It is hilarious how much of a perf tank it is when you switch to that screen 😂 (it's very placeholder, we're baking it into the build later on - just needed a proof of concept with all these different data assets, and this was the best way for the minute x) )

Overall I feel it's worth noting that BPs and C++ work in tandem really well. If something's complex, write it in C++ and expose it to BP <:shrug:872003499511152690> I enjoy hooking up UI as a mixture between the two especially because designers tend to constantly change their minds, so making it BP-friendly gets you the advantage of being able to teach the designers how to tweak small things themselves haha

— Hanhula in #❔tech-questions❔ (jump)

#🎯│audio-discussions

326 messages from 15 people

This was the audio team's onboarding week for the game jam project (ASGC). On Monday a wave of members introduced themselves. Composers were Tsering, Nate (NateTheMood), Sid (Kunga1928), Luciano (LockedInFish) and Gaurav (gs32768). Jack Gruber (TacticalTaco) joined as a technical sound designer and mix engineer, Clara as an ex-Epic tech audio designer who did Fortnite music systems, Roby (Kararo) as an EDM producer and tech art grad, and Braxtyn Larcade for Foley. exackley (James), who leads audio with Jorge Daniel and Dad's Last Nerve, welcomed everyone and said there would be work for every experience level. He also floated building a complex music/ambience system. Much of the week went to sorting access to Google Docs, ClickUp, the GitHub intake form (handled by Seth/VibeBlind) and the LFS server. A When2Meet was used to schedule the first Audio sync up on Friday, July 24 (12–2pm Pacific). There was a brief time zone mix-up for Tsering, and people shared timestamp tools.

The group had a lively DAW discussion: Reaper was widely recommended, and Tsering decided to learn it. Nate proposed bending the 'fantasy' style away from pure orchestral toward charango, gamelan and other world instruments. He then shared a well-received woodwind/kalimba/shamisen style sketch. On VO, the current plan is gibberish 'gabble' speech in the style of Zelda or Animal Crossing rather than recorded voice acting. exackley and TacticalTaco cited time, resources and localization concerns, though Dad's Last Nerve said it isn't set in stone. Clara suggested melodic vocal chops, with characters based on musical modes. After the sync, exackley shared leads-meeting notes: 3–5 greybox levels that get smaller as you go deeper, the loss of the Art lead and some other leadership, and leads being retitled 'Director'.

Like writing stops being a question of "is this good?" and starts being "does this do what I want it to for the game to work?"

— Alex Connelly in #🎯│audio-discussions (jump)

Even though I'm not a composer, what I'd say is balance your learning with 1 part things you are comfortable and 1 part the opposite. So if you have a style or genre you love to write for, do that half the time and the other half, pick something way out of your comfort zone. One problem I've see with composers that start off doing just what they love, then decide to make a living on it, is working on something you don't like or taking direction from someone else can be hard. Learning how to do those things is as or more important than the base trade of writing beautiful music. imo:)

— exackley in #🎯│audio-discussions (jump)

Leads also clarified the 8-hour/24-hour task rule: tasks should be up to 8h or less, and unfinished work goes back to the pool or is handed off with documentation. Pods work on features rather than single tasks. The outdated Miro walkthrough video was removed because the project moved to ClickUp, and Miro is now view-only overview material.

The biggest thread was ClickUp access. Invites are sent manually per department after members submit their department's access form, so they took a while (ThePokePreston sent the Design invites himself). Everyone is a Limited Member by design. Many people hit 'This page is not available' errors, endless loading on sign-up, and a broken public Wiki link. Impyral traced the visibility problems to a new workflow structure and the public sharing being turned off. YosephAlex4nder posted read-only public links to the Wiki pages (Welcome, Rules, NDA, Use of AI, Disciplines/Leadership, Game Jam Concept). Workarounds included re-logging, switching browsers or turning off privacy extensions, and using the mobile app. By Fri Jul 24, ThePokePreston said the team had figured out how to roll out public docs, and the wrong link in announcements was awaiting an update from Lori. Other tips covered installing Unreal 5.7.4, the Gmail dots issue with ClickUp's Google Sign-In, and ways to publicly journal your jam experience.

** Self Care Note**

This is gonna be a long week! It's entirely reasonable to give yourself and others a schedule of when you'll be available and when you won't be available! There might be a desire to make yourself available 24/7, but this is a marathon Game Jam, not a sprint. Try to setup some boundaries so you don't burn out.

— AaronBurrSir in #🗓️│production-lead-discussion (jump)

in my best 'Brain from The Animaniacs' voice Same thing we do every day, Pinky. Try and take over THE WORLD!"

— Daniel Beahn in #🗓️│production-lead-discussion (jump)

I'll write up an FAQ on switching. In addition to the additional time required to onboard someone switching to a new department, there are other impacts. Yes, one of the goals is to learn. Another is to ship a game. Those two are not mutually exclusive, though they do influence each other in ways that we need to be mindful of - if everyone is swapping to something new every sprint, we won't be able to ship. If a single person is swapping every month, the only credit we can give them is "Additional Contributor" (which will be less helpful for a job search than "Associate (whatever)")

I have been thinking about the game pillars. And I think it's kinda fun how we ended up with a steampunk setting. My association with steampunk is that it's all about the industrial revolution, or at least what it represented: an acceleration of technological advances, which causes a major shift in work ethic and urbanization (humans exploit their dominance over nature). Very relevant themes in this day and age. It's kinda the wild west with how fast LLMs are being pushed into our lives. So I think it's a cool setting and theme to play with and mirror our own anxieties.

The coziness of it all, I think, lies in the art of gathering/uniting and the excitement of possibilities and adventure that awaits with the deregulation and chaos of rapid societal changes.

I think the underwater part of the assignment is exciting as well and fits neatly. A large portion of the underwater landscape is uncharted. We can play with unknown ecosystems and species. The environment is a mystery in itself.

What are other people's associations with the prompt?

Notable messages

Producer = 2 parts project manager, 1 part leader, with the occasional dash of clergy. You've got to build your leadership skills because it is your job to to keep your team(s) moving forward, and you aren't writing their annual reviews at the end of the year, so you've got no actual role power over them. The "clergy" bit just means that sometimes team members need to talk through things, and being a good listener and knowing how to ask coaching questions goes a loooooong way towards letting people work through things 🙂

— Daniel Beahn in #🎯│production-discussions (jump)

This is the first time a project like this has been attempted (to the best of my knowledge), and there were gaps in the planning. That means that the discipline leads have been working 20-40 hours a week since we got here in late June, and the same is true for a lot of the deputies, who got here not long after we did.

Those senior folks also have never worked together as a team, and one thing you'll learn about the gaming industry is that there is very little in the way of standardization from one studio/company to another. Even the word "Producer" means something different at EA than it does at Riot, which are both different from what it means at Blizzard, so we're all having to agree on which processes, frameworks, etc. we're going to use.

Patience, grasshopper. In not too long you'll be hip deep in the cat herding that is being a producer 🙂

— Daniel Beahn in #🎯│production-discussions (jump)

I'm not one of the leads-y people, but to my understanding, design is largely about what is going to be in the game and why it should be there, and programming is about how those elements get implemented my classic analogy has always been that a programmer makes Mario jump, but a designer asks "how high?"

— TreeckoRalts in #🎯│production-discussions (jump)

Wait, other disciplines had introduction channels and we don't?! Even here, Producers are second class citizens!

Carl, do something! This is an outrage! Mongo is appalled!

— Daniel Beahn in #🎯│production-discussions (jump)

#SPRINT 1: Prototype Loading Screen!

94 messages from 15 people

Zef kicked off Sprint 1 with a light, team-bonding assignment: a "crowd-sourced" prototype loading screen built telephone/popcorn style. Each artist adds something to a shared PSD (1920x1080, 300dpi, 8-bit), spending about an hour on it. They then rename the file with the next number and their initials, upload it to the Google Drive folder, post a screenshot and tag @Art or the next person. The relay started Saturday Jul 25 at 8:04am. The order ran Nuxe (Natalia) → ŚwAlex (city sketch in the background) → Venom/Tristan (gear crab and coral cave) → Robert Meeks → VarunP (roughed-out seafloor) → BubbleBee (a shark, judging by later messages). Zef then cleaned the file up and split it into foreground, middle and background layer groups. After that came NICK! (the steampunk crab "crabby"), chrissy (the diver), Vaporstar4x4 (a patchwork stingray & friend) and Brandon Trabon (a small hidden addition, made in Krita).

The biggest hiccup was technical. Venom couldn't open the layered PSD in Sketchbook Pro. ŚwAlex suggested a fallback: paint over a flat image and send just your layer. Zef fixed the real problem by changing the bit depth / removing extra channel layers, which made the file work in Sketchbook and Procreate. Zef also said people may merge others' layers if they hit layer limits, since the originals are saved. Other issues: VarunP hit a Photoshop bug where new layers only painted flat blue and worked around it in Clip Studio, and Photoshop masking didn't carry over to Procreate, so the scuba diver kept "dissolving." CamCamtehCamsta, Smetch_D and Nitro plan to take their turns tomorrow (Sunday), and the team will check after the weekend to see who's left.

Highlights - Zef posted the Sprint 1 brief: a yearbook-style, relay-drawn loading screen with file specs and a naming convention (#_LoadingScreen_Initials.psd). - The relay kicked off Saturday morning, and Nuxe delivered the first addition about an hour later. - Venom couldn't open the layered PSD in Sketchbook Pro; Zef fixed it by changing the bit depth, which also made it Procreate-friendly. - Venom added a gear crab and coral cave, which NICK! later turned into a steampunk crab called "crabby." - VarunP worked around a Photoshop bug (new layers only painted flat blue) by finishing in Clip Studio, and roughed out the seafloor as a base. - Photoshop masks didn't survive the move to Procreate, so the scuba diver kept "slowly dissolving" from one handoff to the next. - Zef reorganized the PSD into foreground, middle and background layer groups to make later additions easier. - chrissy reworked the diver, Vaporstar4x4 added a patchwork stingray & friend, and Brandon Trabon slipped in a hidden contribution using Krita.

Topics: Sprint 1 loading screen relay, File specs & naming conventions, Software compatibility (Sketchbook Pro, Procreate, Photoshop, Krita), PSD bit depth & layer issues, Underwater/steampunk scene art, Turn order & handoffs

Open questions - Venom's request to confirm the PSD was uploaded correctly ("I think I uploaded the psd right") got no reply. - VarunP offered to restore the scuba diver's outline he semi-erased, and suggested BubbleBee overlay his flat image under her shark layer; neither offer got a direct answer. - NICK! offered to share backup ideas, but no one took him up on it. - CamCamtehCamsta, Smetch_D and Nitro still have turns pending for Sunday, and remaining participants will be checked after the weekend. - Zef is still looking into better long-term solutions for Sketchbook Pro compatibility.

#FDS-003 - Oxygen System

86 messages from 10 people

LordReavan kicked off the FDS-003 Oxygen System thread as feature lead, and the week turned into a big brainstorm on how to make oxygen feel "cozy." His working concept is a zones system: a central breathable drop zone that expands as players complete surrounding non-breathable areas, borrowing Astroneer's forgiving, progression-driven approach. YellowPie framed the core question as "why does time away from oxygen need to be limited?", using Astroneer, Stardew Valley and Outer Wilds as comparisons. On UI, underwolf pitched a diegetic Dead Space-style display, which grew into four mini canisters on the suit's back. YellowPie's sketch reimagined these as deflating "balloon tanks," and LordReavan likened them to diaphragms or steampunk hot air balloons. Skullbuster suggested a needle gauge, MM a retro digital journal device, and Son of Space a steampunk rebreather with an indicator light. Matt Barnes flagged heavy overlap with Narrative, Art, Animation and Environment, and said refilling must not involve clunky animations; a quick swap station with four hoses was floated.

On failure states, people suggested a tether line back to the last zone (Skullbuster), a teleport back to safety (Wezkal, MM), and oxygen draining only while moving (Orlos). Matt Barnes pushed back on teleport and reset systems because of the project's avoidance of resets. He prefers a stamina-like model: low constant drain, extra drain for actions such as stealth (e.g. x1.5), reduced abilities when empty, and replenishment through bubble pods, air vents and items. LordReavan worried that heavy slowdowns would be uncozy and proposed heavier breathing that hurts stealth instead. ShifuDaxiongmao suggested an experimental backup tank, which LordReavan found too complex. The thread ended on Matt's open challenge to avoid the "tedious walk back." Ideas so far include environmental movement aids, citizens' travel routes, player-built small breathable zones near POIs, and giving players reasons to return to districts. Much of the work is blocked on Narrative, which is still onboarding, especially on defining what "steampunk" means in this setting. LordReavan is updating the Core GDD page with the ideas.

Highlights - LordReavan is leading the Oxygen feature and proposed expandable breathable "zones" centered on the player's drop area. - YellowPie compared how Astroneer, Stardew Valley and Outer Wilds each use a depleting resource, asking what oxygen should do for this game. - The diegetic UI idea evolved from a Dead Space-style bar to four canisters and then to steampunk "balloon tanks"/diaphragms that deflate, eject or pop with smoke when empty. - Matt Barnes ruled out teleport/reset-style fail states and favored a stamina-like system with reduced abilities and in-world replenishment (bubble pods, air vents). - Low baseline drain plus extra drain for actions like stealth, combat, or clearing kelp gained support as "movement as a resource." - Matt Barnes said refills shouldn't involve long animations; a quick refill station with four hoses was suggested. - ShifuDaxiongmao raised that "steampunk" (power source, tech level) must be defined first; LordReavan has contacted Narrative, the biggest dependency. - Son of Space proposed an environment-fed steampunk rebreather and player-built breathable zones near POIs, and LordReavan encouraged pursuing the latter.

Topics: Cozy oxygen design, Breathable zones system, Diegetic UI, Oxygen tank visuals, Fail states and resets, Action-based oxygen drain, Refill stations and replenishment, Steampunk lore definition, Cross-team dependencies

Open questions - How will oxygen be replenished, and how far apart are districts? (MM) - How can the game avoid the "tedious walk back" when oxygen runs out? (Matt Barnes) - Do citizens travel between walkable zones, and can recovery be tied to that? (ShifuDaxiongmao) - What does "steampunk" mean in this setting, e.g. power source and tech level? (waiting on Narrative) - How does Narrative feel about an optional ocean cleanup path? (MM was asked to check) - Should combat actions cost oxygen? (to be raised in the Combat FDS thread) - How should a story reason introduce the protagonist's new O2 tech without feeling clunky? - Will there be a map system to help plan oxygen use?

Notable messages

I think the question we're approaching is "why does time away from oxygen need to be limited?" In Astroneer, it encourages progression: Improving your oxygen infrastructure rewards you the ability to expand the size of your "home base," and improving your ability to carry oxygen rewards you with the ability to explore further from home. In Stardew Valley, another "cozy" game that has a similar system (it's energy rather than oxygen, but it's still a resource that steadily depletes, punishes you for running out, and makes you go out of your way to refill), uses it to put a deadline on all your work: You can max out your farm in a month or clear out the mines in a month, but you can't do both. In Outer Wilds, an exploration-focused game with an oxygen system, it keeps you moving: You can't keep throwing yourself at the same puzzle for too long; you need to step away and get oxygen, and maybe something else will catch your eye on the way (and of course ||the sun explodes|| for a similar reason)

— YellowPie in #FDS-003 - Oxygen System (jump)

Unless there is a very spicific lore reason to allow for this, it conflicts with our avoidance of reset systems. I would prefer an approach that when oxygen is low/empty you just walk slower or cant swim and have to walk or something like that until you can rebuild your resources.

— Matt Barnes in #FDS-003 - Oxygen System (jump)

I think having things in exploration that help motivate the player back towards the activities in the breathable zones will also help a lot. If the player consistently has a destination back in a district when they run out of oxygen it will feel much less like a reset and more like an excuse to go engage with that part of the system.

— Son of Space in #FDS-003 - Oxygen System (jump)

The benefits are good, but I worry that only depleting while moving could actually make you feel too in-control, in a way. If movement costs oxygen, players might feel pressured to optimize their movements to minimize oxygen loss. I suppose it'll depend on just how tight of a resource oxygen is, but something to keep in mind

— YellowPie in #FDS-003 - Oxygen System (jump)

Images - YellowPie's sketch of a deflating "balloon tank" oxygen concept (the one with the burger) - Steampunk hot air balloon reference for mesh-wrapped oxygen tanks

#Add some 400 peeps to GitHub

The channel tackled the chore of manually inviting ~400 jam participants to the ASGC-Game-Jam GitHub org teams. Volunteers (dbugslayer, CrazyRogueWolf, Xhemoz, dyzzy) initially split the intake spreadsheet into batches of 5 rows each, while VibeBlind wrestled with GitHub team permissions (they don't cascade, so maintainers had to be added to every team). Meanwhile pullipulli adapted thundergolfer's automated-github-organization-invites tool into a self-service site hosted on Heroku, letting people add themselves to the right team; VibeBlind confirmed the bot works and on Jul 23 said manual help with the list was no longer needed.

On the Gitea side, Adrian first planned to bulk-create accounts via the Gitea API with random passwords (pullipulli noted exposing an admin token in the frontend would be insecure), then instead set up GitHub as SSO on the Gitea instance at https://asgc.freemyip.com/. pullipulli discovered that GitHub-SSO users must set a local Gitea password (Settings → Account) before Git LFS authentication works; VibeBlind posted the steps and planned to update the SETUP.md page. Gitea usernames match GitHub usernames. By Jul 25 the consensus was that both GitHub and Gitea onboarding are self-service.

Highlights - dbugslayer offered to automate the invite workflow, and pullipulli surfaced thundergolfer's automated-github-organization-invites repo as a starting point. - VibeBlind found GitHub team permissions don't cascade and had to add volunteer maintainers to every team individually. - Volunteers agreed to split intake-sheet rows into batches of 5; dbugslayer claimed rows 65–70. - The invite bot was confirmed working on Heroku, with pullipulli setting it up so people can add themselves to their team. - Adrian enabled 'Sign in using GitHub' SSO on the Gitea instance instead of bulk-creating accounts. - GitHub-SSO users must set a local Gitea password before Git LFS auth works; VibeBlind posted the steps and planned a SETUP.md update. - VibeBlind announced on Jul 23 that the bot handles the list and manual help is no longer needed. - pullipulli plans to publish the self-service tool on GitHub for future jams.

#❔art-questions❔

55 messages from 22 people

The first week of #❔art-questions❔ was mostly onboarding. sevenn4 asked about mentoring. Xelnath said the team was busy getting participants settled, but calendar invites and in-game-jam group calls were expected. On tools, Zef said artists can work in whatever software they're comfortable with, as long as the output works in Unreal and the final build. Impyral shared the creative direction: an underwater setting, a steampunk theme that leans functional, and a mystery story that is creepy or unusual rather than gory. Several people (Venom, Brandon Trabon, Yamz) couldn't open the onboarding tools-access form. Zef said access fixes and setup instructions would come in Art-announcements.

Swart Tigerhorn explained how UI/UX is split. Design writes the specs, storyboards, workflows and accessibility plans. Art owns the visual identity and makes the image files. Tech builds and animates the UI in Unreal. He also noted there's no dedicated technical-art department, but tech won't gatekeep code, and work will happen in multidisciplinary pods put together by Feature Leads. Zef encouraged people to download Unreal, Krita, Blender and similar tools and learn how their assets move through the pipeline. He also said people switching disciplines are welcome to connect early. At Anki's request, Devang Parikh agreed to add timestamps to meetup announcements, and said calls (including Friday's) will be recorded with notes shared. Zef committed to keeping important links in the announcements channel.

Ok show of hands, who thought of bioshock when you heard the description of the art style?

— Jake Goodheart in #❔art-questions❔ (jump)

Hello and welcome!

UI/UX is a multidisciplinary team in our project. It is divided as follows.

Design -> These guys will create the specification documents, create storyboard, workflows, and make all decision around what is shown on screen, when it is shown, how it is shown. Also Accessibility.

Art -> Is going to use the design document to create the necessary image files. Note that art OWNS the visual identity, design will not be making choices like colors, shape, etc.

Tech -> Responsible for creating, coding, and animating the UI Assets inside Unreal Engine. These will closely follow the design specifications and will feature Art deliverables.

See which of those more closely align to what you would like to do UX-wise.

— Swart Tigerhorn in #❔art-questions❔ (jump)

If you want to download unreal, krita, blender and/or any other software you think you'd like to use, you're more than welcome to. The trick for Art Team at least is to make sure what we ship out is fully compatible with the next step in the pipeline with as little hiccups as possible. Knowing how your assets move from the program you like to the program its going to will only help you

— Zef in #❔art-questions❔ (jump)

oh this is huge, that way people on other planets like me won't miss anything

— Anki in #❔art-questions❔ (jump)

#❔audio-questions❔

42 messages from 9 people

The audio team spent the week nailing down delivery conventions and early Unreal Engine workflow questions. Dad's Last Nerve confirmed that every individual file (not just a zip) must follow the naming conventions, and that final deliverables are 48kHz/24-bit WAVs. WIPs can be shared as mp3s on Discord. Carlos Ortiz suggested FLAC to save file size, but WAV stays for now because some tools can't bounce FLAC. TacticalTaco pointed out that Unreal converts imports to 16-bit without dithering. exackley agreed, saying it "should be 16bit to be safe," and will find someone to update the spec. Dad's Last Nerve said the final call depends on the team's skillset and on input from audio QA.

On implementation, composers who want to work in Unreal will be able to as the team takes shape. Tsering and NateTheMood both showed interest. Clara offered to teach UE audio and to tag-team a feature with Tsering. Clara also proposed a list of UE audio plugins to enable. exackley approved it, added AudioWidgets, and was lukewarm on Harmonix, while Clara argued it helps with MIDI timing and beat matching in MetaSounds. Task tracking will happen in ClickUp, alongside an internal audio asset list, but several people (Clara, TacticalTaco, Tsering) were still waiting on invites. Learning resources were shared: Dan Reynolds and Brian Michael Fuller's YouTube channels, plus exackley's Google Doc, which is meant to be the single place for learning links.

Highlights - Individual WAV files inside a zip must also follow the naming conventions. - Final audio deliverables are 48kHz/24-bit WAVs; FLAC was set aside because not all tools can export it, though file size may be revisited. - exackley agreed with TacticalTaco that, since Unreal converts imports to 16-bit without dithering, the spec should be 16-bit to be safe, and will look for someone to update it. - Clara proposed a set of UE audio plugins; exackley approved and added AudioWidgets, while Clara made the case for Harmonix for MIDI timing in MetaSounds. - Composers interested in implementation will get chances to learn it, and Clara offered to teach and tag-team with Tsering. - Task tracking will use ClickUp plus an internal audio asset list, but ClickUp invites hadn't gone out yet. - exackley sees a chance to blend music and world ambience into one smart MetaSounds system, and Clara suggested a music brainstorming session. - Learning resources were collected: Dan Reynolds and Brian Michael Fuller videos, plus exackley's shared Google Doc.

#❔production-questions❔

41 messages from 13 people

A week of onboarding questions from new producers joining the Game Jam production team, answered mostly by Impyral. Producers play a supportive role in general onboarding, and the Public Onboarding Schedule sheet was only for lead sign-ups before public entry on Jul 20. Time isn't tracked per task. The only rule is a 24-hour window to claim a task and then either complete it or return it to the pool with notes. Overall task progress lives in ClickUp. Everyone needs to submit the Tools Access Request for Production form, and the goal is to be ready in tools and processes by 08.01.

Producers will later be assigned to support disciplines, helping with task completion, reporting and risks/blockers, but that assignment process hasn't started yet. For now the job is to learn the Game Jam production process from the ClickUp doc, especially the process flows near the bottom. There's no general Production meeting yet, though a mostly administrative Production Leads/Deputies meeting runs on Sundays. The second half of the week was ClickUp access trouble: missing Spaces, empty workspaces and pending invites. ThePokePreston explained that invites are sent manually, YosephAlex4nder said permissions are still being sorted, and Cafedunoir posted an update in the announcement channel on Jul 24.

Highlights - Impyral clarified that producers support general onboarding, and the Public Onboarding Schedule sheet was only for leads before Jul 20's public entry. - There's no per-task time tracking: claim a task, then complete it or return it to the pool with notes within 24 hours; tasks are sized to fit that window. - The Tools Access Request for Production form was shared, with a goal of being ready in tools and processes by 08.01. - Producers will eventually be assigned to support disciplines; for now everyone should study the Game Jam production process doc in ClickUp. - There's no general Production meeting yet; a mostly administrative Production Leads/Deputies meeting happens on Sundays. - Several members hit ClickUp permission problems; ThePokePreston explained that invites are manual, and YosephAlex4nder said permissions are being sorted. - Members were asked to set their nicknames to 'Name (Discipline) (Timezone)'. - Cafedunoir posted a ClickUp status update in the announcement channel on Jul 24.

— Laura in #Eve Pioche - Community and Support lead with a writer's hat (jump)

I like to think of impostor syndrome as the nagging brain-brother that wants you to prove it wrong but doesn't actually want that to happen.

You: working on some writing... Brainbrother: "You gotta ask yourself one question: does this thing read good? Well, does it, punk?" You: "Ok fine, I guess let's just summarize our goals, show it to someone, and ask them how they felt about it so we know if we're hitting our goals or not." Brainbrother: "W-wait, no, let's not--"

— Luolang in #Abdullah Yusuf - Narrative Designer (jump)

@Laura and @biestibelzer as promised:

The Burning Wheel by Luke Crane is one of the most unique RPGs I've had the pleasure of both running and playing. It's an RPG that is designed to help create interesting stories and characters, as relationships, character motivations, and character goals are built directly into the game rules and players are systematically motivated to both play into and develop their characters. The game has a systemic and natural flow for player action that feeds into upcoming sessions, so GMing is a more dynamic and interactive experience as a lot of the game is directly impacted by player decisions and actions. It also has in my opinion the best implementation of the Tolkien-esque character species such as Orc, Dwarves, and Elves by making them highly distinct from playing humans other than just a reskin: each "stock" has a certain archetypal attribute not available to other ones. For example, Elves have Grief, which can be used beneficially for various tests and rolls. However, if an Elf's Grief ever reaches 10, they cease to be a playable character, as they no longer desire to have anything further to do with the mortal world. This makes the experience of playing an Elf a highly evocative and memorable experience that's very different from choosing to play another stock.

Blades in the Dark by John Harper is probably one of the most influential and important RPGs of the last decade, just as Powered by the Apocalypse was before it (and it itself is an extension of PBTA). The game is designed in a very slick fashion to help create and run adventures as criminals performing heists together in a setting highly reminiscent of the Dishonored universe. However, the core game design extends to tons of other games, particularly in the game's use of creating more granular and interesting task resolution outcomes via action rolls with Position and Effect in mind (essentially, establishing risk and reward to modulate success/failure outcomes), and the use of "Clocks." Clocks can be seen as a successor system to skill challenges in D&D 4e and the like, which divide up individual tasks into multiple chunks. Unlike skill challenges however, Clocks are far more flexible and dynamic in design, play, and use, and are very easily extensible into other kinds of games. Highly recommend playing or at least reading this for some very solid design insights.

Star Wars FFG by Fantasy Flight Games and the generics Genesys RPG system is another highly unique RPG to both play and run. It's a dice pool game, but rather than using d20s, you utilize special dice to help produce varied and unique outcomes for both success and failure. Its Narrative Dice system essentially creates a range of possible outcomes beyond just success/failure, via symbols such as Advantage, Threat, Triumph, or Despair on die faces to effectively modify both potential success or failure. The system was originally introduced for purposes of the Star Wars IP, but FFG has extended it into its own generic system with Genesys, and I'd highly recommend checking it out.

— Luolang in #Abdullah Yusuf - Narrative Designer (jump)

😮 a TTRPG Session for ND by ND

— biestibelzer in #Abdullah Yusuf - Narrative Designer (jump)

It's really fun gameplay wise (essentially 2D Sekiro), but the characters and the overall themes of the story are what really stuck with me personally. It has a really nice narrative device involving the protagonist and an NPC tied to them that pays off incredibly well at the penultimate act of the game. I generally don't find myself getting attached to the characters of most soulslike, but this one did a really job of getting me invested into their stories.

— Luolang in #Abdullah Yusuf - Narrative Designer (jump)

#FDS-008 - Dialogue

18 messages from 4 people

Matt Barnes spun up the #FDS-008 thread as a home for conversations about the Dialog feature, looping in KyotoRando (Clayton) and Oldladies7, who he expects will have some oversight on it. jephph (Jeff) volunteered to help, drawing on dialogue UX work from another project, and posted a train-ride list of design questions: the makeup of the art team and how NPCs are characterized, how locked-in conversations are (Bethesda/BioWare full pause vs. Chants of Sennaar-style floating dialogue boxes), whether there are dialogue choices and if they gate progress, whether dialogue flows into puzzles or shops, the need for teletype, and dialogue overlaid on cinematic or scripted events.

Clayton welcomed Jeff and said none of those questions have answers yet, but they are mostly ones he has raised himself. He offered to share his design doc on the feature, with the caveat that the work may well hit a dead end. Even so, ideas could go into a folder of "neat things to try" for later. Clayton also noted the direction will need teamwork across Design, Narrative and Creative. Oldladies7 shared a rough draft, the "WIP Potential Narrative Feature List" Google Doc, and asked for feedback and a scope check in the doc or its comments. Jeff said he would review it and wait to hear Clayton's thoughts. Nothing was formally decided; the thread is in exploration mode.

Highlights - Matt Barnes set up the thread as the space for Dialog feature discussions and pinged Oldladies7, who he expects will have some oversight on it. - jephph offered to bring dialogue UX experience from another project to the feature. - jephph listed key open design questions: art team makeup, how locked-in conversations are, dialogue choices, ties to puzzles and shops, teletype, and cinematic overlays. - KyotoRando said none of these questions have answers yet and offered to share his design doc on the feature. - KyotoRando framed the exploration as likely to dead-end, but worth keeping in a folder of "neat things to try out" for later. - KyotoRando said the direction will need teamwork between Design, Narrative and Creative. - Oldladies7 shared a rough "WIP Potential Narrative Feature List" doc and asked for feedback and a scope check.

Topics: Dialogue system design, Dialogue UX, Narrative features, Cross-team collaboration, Design docs

Open questions - How locked-in should conversations be: full game pause, floating dialogue boxes, or something in between? - Will there be dialogue choices, and does asking the right things affect progression? - Should dialogue flow into other interactions like puzzles or shops? - Is teletype text needed? - Will dialogue be overlaid on cinematic or scripted events? - What is the art team makeup (2D specialists vs. animators and modelers), and how will NPC characterization be conveyed? - KyotoRando offered to send jephph his dialogue design doc; it hasn't been shared in the thread yet. - Feedback and a scope check on Oldladies7's WIP Potential Narrative Feature List are still pending.

Notable messages

Some things we might want to consider for dialogue. Subject to change and addition cuz I’m just thinking on the train rn:

  • what the makeup of the art team is. Do we have more 2D specialists vs animators and character modelers? How do we want to convey NPC characterization
  • how locked-in is conversation? Are we talking Bethesda/Bioware full-game pause? Something like Chants of Senaar where we mostly just get dialogue boxes in the air? Or something in between
  • how extensive are dialogue choices? Are there any? Is asking the right stuff important to progress the game, if there are choices
  • do dialogue systems have potential to flow into other interactions like puzzles or shops
  • will we want or need teletype
  • will there be cinematic or scripted events which dialogue may be overlaid onto

#I am having some issues testing locking

17 messages from 3 people

Zalk (GitHub/Gitea user Cirieneve) was unable to lock files with Git LFS. Adrian asked for diagnostics by setting GIT_TRACE and GIT_CURL_VERBOSE and re-running git lfs lock on BP_DoorFrame.uasset. The log showed a 401 Unauthorized response from the Gitea server: "You must have push access to create locks". VibeBlind confirmed Zalk was already on the Tech Team on GitHub. After Adrian made a fix, Zalk's next attempt returned a 409 Conflict, because Adrian (ArmainAP) still held a lock on that same file. Adrian unlocked it, and Zalk then created the lock successfully (201 Created, lock id 6).

Adrian explained the root cause: users also need to be added to the organisation team on the Gitea instance at https://asgc.freemyip.com, not just on GitHub. Locking had only worked for Seth because he has an Admin account, and for Adrian because he used the account that owns the Gitea organisation. Adrian then added everyone who is on a Devs team and already has a Gitea account. Some GitHub members don't have Gitea accounts yet.

Highlights - Adrian had Zalk enable GIT_TRACE and GIT_CURL_VERBOSE to debug a failing git lfs lock. - The trace showed a 401 Unauthorized response: "You must have push access to create locks". - VibeBlind confirmed Zalk was already on the Tech Team on GitHub, so GitHub membership wasn't the problem. - After the fix, a 409 Conflict showed the file was already locked by Adrian (ArmainAP), who then unlocked it. - Zalk successfully locked BP_DoorFrame.uasset (201 Created, lock id 6). - Root cause: users must also be on the organisation team on Gitea (asgc.freemyip.com); it had only worked for admins and the org owner. - Adrian added all Gitea accounts belonging to Devs team members, but not everyone on GitHub has a Gitea account yet.

#Pruthvi Das - Trying to roll a Nat 20 in RPGs, tabletop, and turn-based experiences as a writer

16 messages from 6 people

Pruthvi Das introduced himself in his intro thread: 6 years of industry experience, 2 of them as a game writer in India, inspired by Amy Hennig's Uncharted and by The Last of Us (2016) to pursue narrative design. He shared four lessons: localization needs pre-production planning, a good story must be defended against bad direction, the job is about facilitating fantasy even without a formal story, and it teaches you to take rejection in stride. Members responded warmly and asked follow-up questions about localization, Perfect Blue, and how location affects job availability.

Pruthvi described managing a messy localization database of 4,000+ rows. Two years in, the team found that Unity's localization plugin already did what he had wished for. On regional opportunities, he said game writing roles in India are nearly non-existent: he has seen only 2 in 3 years, and he walked away from a recent interview after spotting red flags. That leaves freelancing (Upwork), work abroad (with visa and degree hurdles), OTT/microdramas, or stage plays. biestibelzer added that in Germany, roles often blend ND, game writing and localization, pay is low, and indies often disregard narrative. Ashrah Coliary bonded with Pruthvi over Uncharted 3 and TLoU being shared inspirations. Laura and Pruthvi discussed Satoshi Kon's Perfect Blue, and Laura recommended Black Swan and Tokyo Godfathers. No formal decisions were made.

Highlights - Pruthvi shared four lessons from his writing career, including that a good story needs defending against bad direction. - His localization setup grew from 4,000+ rows in two spreadsheet tabs into something better, and Unity's localization plugin was only discovered two years later. - Pruthvi has seen only 2 game writing roles posted in India in 3 years, and he declined a recent interview after finding red flags. - biestibelzer described the German indie scene as disregarding narrative, underpaying, and expecting several jobs from one hire. - Ashrah Coliary and Pruthvi found they share the same origin story: Uncharted 3 drew them to the industry and TLoU to narrative design. - Laura and Pruthvi discussed how Perfect Blue keeps viewers unsure what is real, and Laura recommended Black Swan and Tokyo Godfathers. - Shrawrdan mentioned getting the TLoU game scripts as a Christmas gift.

Topics: Introductions, Localization pipelines, Game writing jobs in India, Narrative roles in Germany/EU, Uncharted & The Last of Us, Satoshi Kon / Perfect Blue, Career alternatives (OTT, stage plays, freelancing)

Open questions - Pruthvi asked whether Germany's wargaming fanbase should create demand for content designers, as Paradox has; biestibelzer's reply was general and didn't address it directly. - Pruthvi hopes to learn more from a localization specialist he believes is in the server; no one has stepped forward yet.

Notable messages

Despite having 6 years of industry experience, I've been a writer for video games only 2 years, given its rarity in India. While on paper I've done it professionally for that long, the responsibility of wearing the writing hat came to me naturally with any gamedev opportunity I landed.

My lucky number’s 4, so if I had to pick 4 writing lessons that left a cattle brand in my mind from my experience, they’d be…

  • Localization needs pre-prod planning because it isn’t just about translating things.
  • Making a good story involves defending it against bad direction.
  • Even if there's no formal story to tell, the job's still about facilitating fantasy.
  • It’s the best job to learn to take life’s rejections in strides, even if it hurts all the same.

I try to bring these learnings to the table to help make games better and compound my own experience.

As a kid, I grew up with action figures, movies, and video games, and they sparked my imagination in ways that gave me the wish to make games that facilitate that experience for others. I didn't know until after playing The Last of Us in 2016 that it meant being a narrative designer. Somehow, it didn’t click that it takes being a writer to get there.

I figured out that I'd want to work with specific people than with companies; one such person being Amy Hennig. Her direction with the Uncharted series inspired me to join the industry in the first place. Saying that… between the state of the industry, and my poor chances of landing anything, I don’t know if that will ever happen… So I’m soothing away the unresolved grief by being more proactive online with people that just want to make games and look after the little guys… Being a friendly neighbourhood game writer, if you will.

Recent Media: - Books: The Kamogawa Food Detectives. Epistolary fiction might be my go-to; throw recs my way. - Films: The Invite, Perfect Blue, Detachment. - Games: Adios, Candellum, Paralives, Signs of the Sojourner, Five Hearts Under One Roof.

— Pruthvi in #Pruthvi Das - Trying to roll a Nat 20 in RPGs, tabletop, and turn-based experiences as a writer (jump)

I don't do translation work, but I took ownership of managing the database, so any time we needed text to go into the game, I could help out with it. So I can only speak in terms of what not to do when trying to set up a localization system for games. Long story short, it was a nightmare. We went from managing 4,000+ rows of text in two tabs to something more fruitful after I came in. 4-5 months into the job, and I was crying about wanting a solution that felt more plug-and-play... 2 years later, it was discovered that Unity had a localization plugin that does everything I cried about; these things are often overlooked when there's simply no time to do research on the technologies + it never comes up in discussions. At least the upside to that experience is now I know how much goes into setting up a system just to parse and translate basic text.

I think I saw at least one person who specializes in it as a core thing in this server, so I'm quite excited to learn a lot more about it as you are.

— Pruthvi in #Pruthvi Das - Trying to roll a Nat 20 in RPGs, tabletop, and turn-based experiences as a writer (jump)

I can only speak from my experience. In India, I'd say in the past 3 years, I've only ever seen 2 writing roles pop up. And when I say "writing role" I mean game writing (and since we often mix narrative design into it, for the sake of brevity I could add that in, too). One was the last full-time job I did (so I seized it, yay), and the other was sus. Any other narrative peeps working in India are likely hiring from within (so they never post about it), or are working on their own game. We do have bigger studios here, but if they're hiring for narrative peeps, they're likely hiring contractors for it.

With that said, it's safe to conclude that game narrative as a scope of career in India is almost non-existent. Which means my only other approach is to apply for jobs outside. For that to go smoothly, I'd have to get a visa (expensive), have a degree (I'm a dropout, but it's part of visa requirements), have a stellar portfolio (I imagine mine's not that great compared to the global market), and make a good case for why I'm worth hiring (considering I'm a neophyte writer, no such case exists).

The last time I saw another full-time writing job in India pop up after that experience was last week, for which I applied. Got a call today about it, and I had an interview scheduled. Then I discovered some really sus things that freaked both me and my mentor out, so I opted not to go ahead.

Upwork/freelancing would be my only other option available, and it seems to work! ... when it does.

— Pruthvi in #Pruthvi Das - Trying to roll a Nat 20 in RPGs, tabletop, and turn-based experiences as a writer (jump)

Oh man, don't get me started about people just mixing ND and Game Writing and sometimes even Localization all into one thing 🥲 Writing roles are rare but it almost feels like there's a bit of a turn up in EU and US lately, but it feels like only senior level sometimes. The German indie scene either disregards writing and ND, already have one or just plain don't have the money or want to spend money on an ND/GW... I've only ever had, or taken, one job in Germany. The others either tried to underpay criminally or expect five jobs out of you, while underpaying. The other stuff was remote/International, and they didn't pay well either 😅 😐 We have some other writing opportunities here but it feels like they are already crammed with the people having gone for school for those and it's almost a little jealous fighting between other creative media and games to let games be at their table.

— biestibelzer in #Pruthvi Das - Trying to roll a Nat 20 in RPGs, tabletop, and turn-based experiences as a writer (jump)

#Production Guidelines for AI Use with Meetings

14 messages from 3 people

AaronBurrSir (Matt) opened a reference thread for leads listing tools for capturing meeting notes. His Producer Guidelines say each producer can choose whether to use generative AI note-taking or summary tools. Meeting minutes, action items and decisions must be recorded for every meeting and shared with the rest of the team. The AI tools listed were Fathom (free unlimited recording and transcription, but AI summaries capped at 5 meetings a month), Google Gemini's "Take Notes for me" in Google Meet (Business Standard plan or higher only) and Zoom AI Assistant (free limits still to be checked). The non-AI option was Realtime STT, a free Python speech-to-text tool that takes more technical skill to set up. Matt also suggested Ollama, which runs LLMs locally, as a compromise for people who want to learn these tools without relying on big data centers.

shadowarrior (Sidds) asked why AI use was being discussed again, since each department settled it at the start of the project. He stressed that minutes, action items and decisions just need to be captured reliably, possibly as part of the meeting monitor role. Matt clarified that the AI question is settled, that Daniel had communicated the Production team's intentions well, and that the thread is only a reference for leads. Matt then updated the guidelines with Sidds' points about minutes and the Google Meet plan requirement. Cafedunoir offered to add the info to the Production wiki/tools, and Matt said he'll keep the wiki updated as he learns more.

Highlights - Matt posted Producer Guidelines: using AI note-taking is each producer's choice, but minutes, action items and decisions must be recorded and shared after every meeting. - The tool list covers Fathom, Google Gemini "Take Notes for me", Zoom AI Assistant and the non-AI Realtime STT Python tool. - Matt suggested Ollama, a locally run LLM, as a lower-impact way to learn AI tools without feeding big data centers. - Sidds questioned reopening the AI topic and said capturing meeting outcomes is what matters, however it's done. - Matt confirmed the AI question is settled and credited Daniel with communicating the Production team's intentions well. - Sidds noted Google Meet note-taking requires a Google Business Standard plan or higher, and Matt updated the list. - Cafedunoir will add the info to the Production wiki/tools, and Matt will keep the wiki updated.

Topics: Meeting minutes and action items, AI note-taking tools, Fathom, Google Meet / Gemini, Zoom AI Assistant, Realtime STT, Ollama local LLM, Production wiki

Open questions - Is the Zoom AI Assistant free version limited? Matt said he'd investigate. - Is Google Meet's transcription/recording-only feature (without "Take Notes for me") free? Matt said he'd look into it. - Does the Fathom individual license allow unlimited automated summaries? - Are there any automatic transcription or note-taking bots for Discord?

Notable messages

Question: AI/LLM usage is the first thing we covered and decided on at the start of the project for each department. Why is this a topic of discussion now?

Meeting minutes, action items from the meeting, decisions taken : these are critical. Can be fused with meeting monitor role for that call. If we can reliably capture that information everytime, I don't care how it is done. There should not be any meetings where the above details aren't captured and communicated out to the rest of the team members.

— shadowarrior in #Production Guidelines for AI Use with Meetings (jump)

I consider the AI question covered and I think Daniel has done a good job communicating the Production teams intentions. This thread is being created so people are aware of the tools available and how they can do things manually if they so choose.

— AaronBurrSir in #Production Guidelines for AI Use with Meetings (jump)

Daniel one thing I am interested in. Ollama is a free LLM model that can be ran entirely locally so the only persons resources being used are your own, and you're avoiding the water/electricity use of big data centers. It could be a compromise for folks that want to learn how to use those tools but don't want to feed into the overall LLM ecosystem.

Our first step is going to be for people to gain an understanding of the type of game we are making.

I do not see community hitting the ground running. There are a lot of unknowns about the project and we all need an understanding of what the game is really going to be.

Lauren (Explaurer) introduced herself to the narrative team: 28, she/her, from the smoke-filled Northeast USA. Notable messages

Heyo narrative fam, I'm Lauren! A/S/L? 28, she/her, glorious smoke filled Northeast USA.

Let's see here... I caught the game narrative bug in 2020 after stumbling my way into an uber talented writer's room for an ambitious mod aiming to overhaul Skyrim's main quest with deeper choices, consequences, and fun new companion characters. Got my first writing paycheck working on the most popular Skyrim follower mod on the interwebs (yes, it's about Serana).

From there, I started joining any game jam that would have me, primarily month long visual novel affairs. One such jam was similar in concept to this one: organize a big group of people to work on a game for Ludum Dare. From that jam, I met some extremely talented ex-industry folks, we hit it off, and eventually had the crazy idea to start our own indie studio. So that's a thing!

Other than that, I recently became a published author in an edited book series exploring the intersection of psychology and pop culture. I'm also the narrative lead on a new origin character mod for Baldur's Gate 3! Oh, I'm back in school with the kiddies finishing up the last year of a bachelor's degree in marketing.
